1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Detection Signal Across Model Families}
5	\label{sec:detection}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:cohens_d} presents Cohen's $d$ effect sizes for $\ell_z^*$ across three model families and two benchmarks.
8	All confidence intervals exclude zero, confirming that $\Delta\ell_z^*$ reliably separates contaminated from clean models at both contamination levels.
9	
10	On MMLU, effect sizes range from $|d|{=}2.30$ (Llama at 50\%) to $|d|{=}7.44$ (Qwen at 25\%), with Qwen at 5\% ($|d|{=}4.15$) confirming detection at the lowest contamination level tested. Llama ($|d|{=}2.80$/$2.56$/$2.30$) and Mistral ($|d|{=}2.64$/$3.50$) show comparable magnitudes.
11	On ARC-C (295 items), Qwen yields $|d|{=}3.16$ at 15\% and $|d|{=}5.30$ at 50\%, demonstrating that detection remains feasible even on small benchmarks when contamination is sufficiently concentrated.
12	
13	Clean-model $\ell_z^*$ baselines diverge substantially across families: Qwen ${+}8.68$ ($\text{SD}{=}0.74$), Mistral ${+}4.02$ ($\text{SD}{=}2.05$), Llama ${+}0.26$ ($\text{SD}{=}1.54$), and ARC-C Qwen ${-}5.42$ ($\text{SD}{=}0.16$); this divergence reflects difficulty-ordering consistency rather than accuracy level (Appendix~\ref{app:baseline_meaning}).
14	Absolute $\ell_z^*$ thresholds would produce contradictory results: Qwen's contaminated $\ell_z^*$ at 15\% (${+}3.8$) is still positive.
15	The $\Delta\ell_z^*$ design normalizes these divergent baselines: Cohen's $d$ yields comparable effect sizes ($|d|{=}2.30$--$7.44$) despite order-of-magnitude differences.
16	Figure~\ref{fig:heatmap} provides a visual summary.
17

158	
159	\subsection{Ability-Dependent Non-Monotonic Dose-Response}
160	\label{sec:inverted_u}
161	
162	As an exploratory observation, the free-$\theta$ dose-response is non-monotonic for Qwen.
163	The multi-seed mean trajectory ($|d|$: $4.15 \to 5.36 \to 7.44 \to 5.08$ at 5\%/15\%/25\%/50\%; Appendix~\ref{app:reproducibility}) shows signal peaking at 25\% before declining at 50\%, but the decline is attenuated relative to single-seed estimates and the inter-seed variance at 50\% is high (CV${=}$60\%, range $[1.90, 7.91]$).
164	This non-monotonicity is a $\theta$-estimation artifact: fixed-$\theta$ analysis eliminates the inverted-U and reveals a strong positive dose-response (mean $|d|$: $5.89$ at 25\% vs.\ $12.32$ at 50\%, no seed-level overlap), while reducing the 50\% CV to 19\%.
165	Llama shows an approximately flat dose-response ($|d|{=}2.80 \to 2.56 \to 2.30$), consistent with its near-zero $\ell_z^*$ baseline ($+0.26$ vs.\ ${+}8.68$ for Qwen) despite comparable $\theta$-shift ($\Delta\theta{=}0.52$ vs.\ $0.47$).
166	The mechanism is predictable from simulation (Appendix~\ref{app:simulation}): at moderate contamination, aberrant responses dominate; at high contamination, inflated $\hat{\theta}$ absorbs the anomaly---but the magnitude is highly seed-dependent.
167	Three regimes are visible in Qwen's free-$\theta$ trajectory: at 5\%, $\theta$-shift is negligible and $\ell_z^*$ captures the full misfit; through 15--25\%, pattern deviations outpace $\theta$-shift absorption, yielding peak signal; at 50\%, $\Delta\theta{=}0.47$ absorbs enough aberrance that $|d|$ declines despite double the contamination fraction.
168	
169	A simulated power analysis corroborates these findings: two-sided testing (flagging both large negative and large positive $\ell_z^*$) achieves ${>}$93\% power on MMLU at all contamination levels, with the theoretical power function yielding $R^2 {=} 0.957$ against simulated values (Appendix~\ref{app:simulation}).
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MoXQTHDPTvWDJHvyir3ZL2`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our controlled experiments cover three 7--8B model families with QLoRA fine-tuning; generalization to larger scales, mixture-of-experts architectures, full-parameter fine-tuning, and pretraining-stage contamination remains untested.
4	Most contamination conditions use a single training run, and multi-seed replication (available for Qwen and Llama) reveals high seed dependence at 50\% contamination (CV${=}$60\%), partially resolved by $\theta$-anchoring; the post-RL characterization is $N{=}1$.
5	The framework depends on pre-calibrated 4PL item parameters from PSN-IRT, whose calibration sample may include contaminated models, and we do not provide head-to-head comparison with logit-based methods (MIA, ConStat) that operate at a different information level.
6	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QSHFA8qCPGG19imd8i5cMx`</summary>

```text
25	The fraction of contaminated items that the model memorizes is 0.38 (15\%) and 0.29 (50\%).
26	SFT generalizes beyond the memorized items, as fewer than half the injected items show rote recall.
27	
28	\paragraph{Cross-family confirmation.}
29	Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct confirms generalizability: $|\text{Cohen's}\;d|{=}2.95$ (15\%) and $3.31$ (50\%) ($n{=}3$ clean seeds, mean$\,{=}\,{-}0.14\pm0.97$), with $|\Delta\ell_z^*|{=}2.85$ (15\%) and $3.19$ (50\%).
30	Unlike Qwen, Llama exhibits monotonic Cohen's $d$ dose-response ($|d|$ increases from 2.95 to 3.31), consistent with its lower baseline misfit.
31	Memorization fractions (0.35/0.33) and fixed-$\theta$ signal ratios (2.87) are consistent across Qwen and Llama.
32	
33	Mistral-7B-Instruct ($n{=}1$ clean seed, $\ell_z^*{=}{+}5.42$) produces the strongest detection signal: $|\Delta\ell_z^*|{=}6.80$ (15\%) and $8.57$ (50\%), with monotonic dose-response.
34	The stronger signal likely reflects Mistral's lower baseline ability ($\text{acc}{=}0.514$, $\hat{\theta}{=}{-}1.16$), which provides greater $\theta$-shift headroom.
35	Because $n{=}1$, we report $\Delta\ell_z^*$ only; Cohen's $d$ requires $n \geq 2$ clean seeds.
36	
37	\paragraph{Cross-family baseline divergence.}
38	Clean $\ell_z^*$ baselines diverge substantially: Qwen $+8.68$, Mistral $+5.42$, Llama $-0.14$.
39	Qwen's contaminated $\ell_z^*$ at 15\% ($+3.84$) remains positive---absolute thresholding would miss it; \irtfit{} detects contamination through the within-family shift $\Delta\ell_z^*{=}{-}4.84$.
```

      "content": "The fraction of contaminated items that the model memorizes is 0.38 (15\\%) and 0.29 (50\\%).\nSFT generalizes beyond the memorized items, as fewer than half the injected items show rote recall.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family confirmation.}\nLlama-3.1-8B-Instruct confirms generalizability: $|\\text{Cohen's}\\;d|{=}2.95$ (15\\%) and $3.31$ (50\\%) ($n{=}3$ clean seeds, mean$\\,{=}\\,{-}0.14\\pm0.97$), with $|\\Delta\\ell_z^*|{=}2.85$ (15\\%) and $3.19$ (50\\%).\nUnlike Qwen, Llama exhibits monotonic Cohen's $d$ dose-response ($|d|$ increases from 2.95 to 3.31), consistent with its lower baseline misfit.\nMemorization fractions (0.35/0.33) and fixed-$\\theta$ signal ratios (2.87) are consistent across Qwen and Llama.\n\nMistral-7B-Instruct ($n{=}1$ clean seed, $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}5.42$) produces the strongest detection signal: $|\\Delta\\ell_z^*|{=}6.80$ (15\\%) and $8.57$ (50\\%), with monotonic dose-response.\nThe stronger signal likely reflects Mistral's lower baseline ability ($\\text{acc}{=}0.514$, $\\hat{\\theta}{=}{-}1.16$), which provides greater $\\theta$-shift headroom.\nBecause $n{=}1$, we report $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ only; Cohen's $d$ requires $n \\geq 2$ clean seeds.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-family baseline divergence.}\nClean $\\ell_z^*$ baselines diverge substantially: Qwen $+8.68$, Mistral $+5.42$, Llama $-0.14$.\nQwen's contaminated $\\ell_z^*$ at 15\\% ($+3.84$) remains positive---absolute thresholding would miss it; \\irtfit{} detects contamination through the within-family shift $\\Delta\\ell_z^*{=}{-}4.84$.",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Across Model Families}\n\\label{sec:detection}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens_d} presents Cohen's $d$ effect sizes for $\\ell_z^*$ across three model families and two benchmarks.\nAll confidence intervals exclude zero, confirming that $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ reliably separates contaminated from clean models at both contamination levels.\n\nOn MMLU, effect sizes range from $|d|{=}2.30$ (Llama at 50\\%) to $|d|{=}7.44$ (Qwen at 25\\%), with Qwen at 5\\% ($|d|{=}4.15$) confirming detection at the lowest contamination level tested. Llama ($|d|{=}2.80$/$2.56$/$2.30$) and Mistral ($|d|{=}2.64$/$3.50$) show comparable magnitudes.\nOn ARC-C (295 items), Qwen yields $|d|{=}3.16$ at 15\\% and $|d|{=}5.30$ at 50\\%, demonstrating that detection remains feasible even on small benchmarks when contamination is sufficiently concentrated.\n\nClean-model $\\ell_z^*$ baselines diverge substantially across families: Qwen ${+}8.68$ ($\\text{SD}{=}0.74$), Mistral ${+}4.02$ ($\\text{SD}{=}2.05$), Llama ${+}0.26$ ($\\text{SD}{=}1.54$), and ARC-C Qwen ${-}5.42$ ($\\text{SD}{=}0.16$); this divergence reflects difficulty-ordering consistency rather than accuracy level (Appendix~\\ref{app:baseline_meaning}).\nAbsolute $\\ell_z^*$ thresholds would produce contradictory results: Qwen's contaminated $\\ell_z^*$ at 15\\% (${+}3.8$) is still positive.\nThe $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ design normalizes these divergent baselines: Cohen's $d$ yields comparable effect sizes ($|d|{=}2.30$--$7.44$) despite order-of-magnitude differences.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} provides a visual summary.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cohen's $d$ for $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ across model families ($n{=}10$ clean seeds). Single-seed conditions: percentile bootstrap 95\\% CI (10,000 resamples). $^{\\dagger}$Multi-seed conditions: mean $\\pm$ SD ($n{=}3$ independent runs). All CIs exclude zero.}\n\\label{tab:cohens_d}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Bench. & $d$ (5\\%) [CI] & $d$ (15\\%) [CI] & $d$ (25\\%) [CI] & $d$ (50\\%) [CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^{\\dagger}$ & MMLU & $-4.15$ [$-9.9$, $-2.7$] & $-5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $-7.44$ ($\\pm$1.86) & $-5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\nLlama$^{\\dagger}$ & MMLU & --- & $-2.80$ ($\\pm$0.60) & $-2.56$ ($\\pm$0.52) & $-2.30$ ($\\pm$0.30) \\\\\nMistral & MMLU & --- & $-2.64$ [$-7.3$, $-1.5$] & --- & $-3.50$ [$-9.4$, $-2.1$] \\\\\nQwen$^{\\dagger}$ & ARC-C & --- & $+3.16$ [$2.0$, $9.5$] & --- & $+5.30$ [$3.6$, $15.2$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$Qwen and Llama MMLU 15\\%/25\\%/50\\%: multi-seed means ($n{=}3$ independent runs); Qwen 5\\%: single seed. Per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Cohen's $|d|$ heatmap (values from Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}; multi-seed means where available). Blue intensity reflects effect-size magnitude. Grey cells: conditions not tested.}\n  \\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Model-Specific $\\theta$-Shift Dynamics}\n\\label{sec:theta_dynamics}\n\nThe cross-family variation in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}---Qwen's $|d|$ declining from 25\\% to 50\\% while Llama's remains flat---suggests detection sensitivity depends on more than contamination dose.\nContamination raises overall accuracy, which inflates $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MLE}}$; a higher $\\hat{\\theta}$ ``explains away'' anomalous successes on hard items, absorbing the contamination signal into the ability estimate.\nWe term this \\emph{$\\theta$-shift absorption}.\nThe magnitude of $\\theta$-shift is comparable across families---Qwen ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ at 50\\%) and Llama ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$)---yet their fixed-$\\theta$ amplification diverges markedly (Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), indicating that $\\theta$-shift magnitude alone does not predict fixed-$\\theta$ benefit.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:theta_scatter} plots baseline accuracy against Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations.\n\n$\\theta$-shift absorption is the dominant source of detection variability for high-ability models at high contamination:\n(i) fixed-$\\theta$ estimation amplifies Qwen's multi-seed $|d|$ by 2.4$\\times$ at 50\\% ($5.08 \\to 12.32$; Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), while Llama shows no amplification ($0.4\\times$);\n(ii) $\\theta$-anchoring reduces Qwen's inter-seed CV from 60\\% to 19\\%, confirming that training-run variance is driven by $\\theta$-shift stochasticity rather than true signal variance;\n(iii) the cross-model ranking under free-$\\theta$ is seed-dependent at 50\\%, making $\\theta$-anchoring a necessary condition for reliable detection of high-ability models at high contamination.\n\nARC-C reverses this pattern.\nDespite Qwen's high baseline accuracy on ARC-C (89.7\\%), contamination produces larger, not smaller, effect sizes ($|d|{=}3.16$ at 15\\%, $|d|{=}5.30$ at 50\\%).\nThis reversal reflects a ceiling effect: with only 295 items and 89.7\\% baseline accuracy, the already-correct items offer minimal room for further $\\theta$ inflation, so contamination manifests primarily as anomalous successes on the few remaining hard items.\nThe small item count amplifies this concentration, producing a disproportionately large $\\ell_z^*$ shift per contaminated item.\n\nThe baseline $\\ell_z^*$ for ARC-C is negative ($-5.42$), reflecting the 4PL model's expectation of near-perfect performance; contamination pushes $\\ell_z^*$ toward zero, producing the positive Cohen's $d$ values in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}.\n\nThese findings indicate that detection sensitivity is not a fixed property of $\\ell_z^*$ but depends on the interaction between model ability and benchmark characteristics, a consideration absent from standard person-fit applications.\n\n\\paragraph{Fixed-$\\theta$ estimation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed} compares free-$\\theta$ and fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $d$ across all three families.\nThe benefit of fixed-$\\theta$ is modulated by baseline $\\ell_z^*$ level rather than $\\theta$-shift magnitude.\nQwen (67.8\\% accuracy, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}8.68$) shows 2.4$\\times$ amplification at 50\\%: its large baseline departure from the IRT null provides substantial signal that free-$\\theta$ absorption erodes, which $\\theta$-anchoring recovers (multi-seed mean ${\\ell_z^*}_{\\text{fixed}} {=} 14.5$ vs.\\ clean $7.35$).\nMistral (52.4\\%, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}4.02$) shows 1.7$\\times$ at 50\\%.\nLlama (59.4\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.39$ at 15\\%, $0.52$ at 50\\%) shows 0.4$\\times$ at 50\\% despite comparable $\\theta$-shift: its near-zero baseline $\\ell_z^*$ ($+0.26$) indicates that the clean response pattern conforms to IRT expectations, leaving no absorbed signal for $\\theta$-anchoring to recover.\nThe amplification ratio tracks baseline $|\\ell_z^*|$ monotonically ($8.68 {\\to} 2.4{\\times}$; $4.02 {\\to} 1.7{\\times}$; $0.26 {\\to} 0.4{\\times}$ at 50\\%), making fixed-$\\theta$ a targeted remedy for models with substantial baseline misfit, not a universal one.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Free-$\\theta$ vs.\\ fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $|d|$ on MMLU (bootstrap 95\\% CI). Fixed-$\\theta$ anchors $\\hat{\\theta}$ at the clean-model mean, eliminating $\\theta$-shift absorption.}\n\\label{tab:theta_fixed}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $|d_{\\text{free}}|$ & $|d_{\\text{fixed}}|$ & Ratio (fixed/free) & Fixed CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{5\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen & 4.15 & 3.52 & 0.8$\\times$ & [2.9, 5.7] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{15\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.36 & 6.85 & 1.3$\\times$ & ($\\pm$1.80) \\\\\nLlama & 2.11 & 0.58 & 0.3$\\times$ & [0, 2.37]$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 2.64 & 2.69 & 1.0$\\times$ & [1.53, 9.06] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{50\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.08 & 12.32 & 2.4$\\times$ & ($\\pm$2.38) \\\\\nLlama & 2.33 & 0.84 & 0.4$\\times$ & [0.17, 2.97] \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 3.50 & 5.80 & 1.7$\\times$ & [3.87, 18.45] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$CI includes zero.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\S}$Multi-seed mean ($n{=}3$); CI column shows $\\pm$SD of fixed-$\\theta$ $|d|$.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Fixed-$\\theta$: 6 seeds (4 response vectors from seeds 4--7 not retained in pipeline); free-$\\theta$ uses all 10.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_theta_scatter.pdf}\n  \\caption{Baseline accuracy vs.\\ Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations (single-seed values; multi-seed means in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}). Diamonds: 5\\%; triangles: 15\\%; squares: 25\\%; circles: 50\\%. ARC-C reversal reflects a ceiling effect.}\n  \\label{fig:theta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Detection-Diagnosis Tradeoff}\n\\label{sec:degradation}\n\nHaving established that $\\theta$-shift complicates detection, we ask whether the full 4PL framework is necessary, or whether simpler statistics suffice.\nTable~\\ref{tab:degradation} compares three statistics at decreasing levels of information access: $\\ell_z^*$ (requires 4PL parameters $a, b, c, d$), the HT statistic (requires only difficulty $b$), and an accuracy $z$-score (requires no item parameters).\n\nThe comparison reveals a detection-diagnosis tradeoff.\nFor pure detection, simpler statistics are more powerful: accuracy $z$-scores yield $|d|$ values several-fold to over an order of magnitude larger than person-fit statistics (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), because cross-seed accuracy variance is near-zero in controlled settings.\nHT generally exceeds $\\ell_z^*$ in detection strength, since HT is not attenuated by the Snijders correction for $\\theta$ estimation uncertainty.\nBoth person-fit statistics exhibit seed-dependent non-monotonicity under free-$\\theta$ estimation (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), confirming that $\\theta$-shift absorption affects all statistics conditioned on ability, not $\\ell_z^*$ specifically.\n\nYet the diagnostic power reverses.\nFull IRT parameterization is unnecessary for mere detection, but the value of $\\ell_z^*$ lies in three capabilities that HT and $z$-scores lack:\n(a) \\emph{seen/unseen decomposition}: $\\ell_z^*$ can be computed separately on seen and unseen item subsets, though item-level detection is near-random (AUROC $\\approx 0.53$; Appendix~\\ref{app:item_auroc}), confirming that the contamination signal is diffuse and requires model-level aggregation;\n(b) \\emph{$\\theta$-shift mechanism}: the ability parameter exposes why detection sensitivity depends on model ability (\\S\\ref{sec:theta_dynamics}), a mechanistic insight inaccessible to non-parametric statistics;\n(c) \\emph{structural sensitivity}: on real leaderboard data, $\\ell_z^*$ flags 57/60 model-benchmark cells as aberrant---evidence of systematic IRT assumption violations that motivates the $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ matched-difference design (\\S\\ref{sec:did}; Appendix~\\ref{app:leaderboard_aberrance}).\nAccuracy $z$-scores flag only 4/60 cells, making them insensitive to the response-pattern structure that $\\ell_z^*$ exploits for within-model diagnosis.\nWhile such patterns have theoretical antecedents in person-fit literature \\citep{stonge2011}, the specific magnitude and model-dependence we observe are new empirical findings in the LLM domain; nonparametric alternatives \\citep{tendeiro2014} may complement $\\ell_z^*$ where 4PL calibration is unavailable.\n\nIn practice, a two-stage workflow follows: screen using accuracy $z$-scores (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), then apply $\\ell_z^*$ to characterize whether the signal reflects $\\theta$-shift absorption (indicating fixed-$\\theta$ anchoring is needed) or uniform aberrance (suggesting direct memorization).\n\nWithin the IRT family, the 4PL specification matters: simulation ablations show a mean $+$27.3pp power gain over 3PL on MMLU, because 46\\% of MMLU items have upper asymptote $d < 0.95$, and the 3PL model (which assumes $d{=}1$) misattributes slip-related response patterns as aberrance (Appendix~\\ref{app:4pl_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection-diagnosis tradeoff on MMLU: Cohen's $|d|$ across three statistics. Simpler statistics detect more powerfully; $\\ell_z^*$ provides unique diagnostic capabilities (\\S\\ref{sec:degradation}).}\n\\label{tab:degradation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Statistic & $|d|$ (15\\%) & $|d|$ (50\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^\\dagger$ & $\\ell_z^*$ & $5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\n     & HT & $5.83$ ($\\pm$1.30) & $5.29$ ($\\pm$3.41) \\\\\n     & Acc.\\ $z$ & $45.0$ ($\\pm$2.5) & $70.0$ ($\\pm$1.1) \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLlama & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.11$ [$1.4$, $4.8$] & $2.33$ [$1.6$, $5.1$] \\\\\n      & HT & $2.45$ [$1.5$, $4.3$] & $3.07$ [$1.9$, $5.3$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $22.5$ [$13.6$, $47.0$] & $32.8$ [$19.8$, $68.6$] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.64$ [$1.5$, $7.3$] & $3.50$ [$2.1$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & HT & $4.36$ [$1.2$, $7.0$] & $5.89$ [$1.9$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $7.81$ [$4.4$, $14.4$] & $16.6$ [$9.7$, $34.6$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.9\\columnwidth}}{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$Qwen values are means across $n{=}3$ independent seeds (42, 123, 456); per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}. Llama and Mistral are single-seed with bootstrap CIs.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ability-Dependent Non-Monotonic Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:inverted_u}\n\nAs an exploratory observation, the free-$\\theta$ dose-response is non-monotonic for Qwen.\nThe multi-seed mean trajectory ($|d|$: $4.15 \\to 5.36 \\to 7.44 \\to 5.08$ at 5\\%/15\\%/25\\%/50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}) shows signal peaking at 25\\% before declining at 50\\%, but the decline is attenuated relative to single-seed estimates and the inter-seed variance at 50\\% is high (CV${=}$60\\%, range $[1.90, 7.91]$).\nThis non-monotonicity is a $\\theta$-estimation artifact: fixed-$\\theta$ analysis eliminates the inverted-U and reveals a strong positive dose-response (mean $|d|$: $5.89$ at 25\\% vs.\\ $12.32$ at 50\\%, no seed-level overlap), while reducing the 50\\% CV to 19\\%.\nLlama shows an approximately flat dose-response ($|d|{=}2.80 \\to 2.56 \\to 2.30$), consistent with its near-zero $\\ell_z^*$ baseline ($+0.26$ vs.\\ ${+}8.68$ for Qwen) despite comparable $\\theta$-shift ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$ vs.\\ $0.47$).\nThe mechanism is predictable from simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}): at moderate contamination, aberrant responses dominate; at high contamination, inflated $\\hat{\\theta}$ absorbs the anomaly---but the magnitude is highly seed-dependent.\nThree regimes are visible in Qwen's free-$\\theta$ trajectory: at 5\\%, $\\theta$-shift is negligible and $\\ell_z^*$ captures the full misfit; through 15--25\\%, pattern deviations outpace $\\theta$-shift absorption, yielding peak signal; at 50\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ absorbs enough aberrance that $|d|$ declines despite double the contamination fraction.\n\nA simulated power analysis corroborates these findings: two-sided testing (flagging both large negative and large positive $\\ell_z^*$) achieves ${>}$93\\% power on MMLU at all contamination levels, with the theoretical power function yielding $R^2 {=} 0.957$ against simulated values (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Across Model Families}\n\\label{sec:detection}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens_d} presents Cohen's $d$ effect sizes for $\\ell_z^*$ across three model families and two benchmarks.\nAll confidence intervals exclude zero, confirming that $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ reliably separates contaminated from clean models at both contamination levels.\n\nOn MMLU, effect sizes range from $|d|{=}2.30$ (Llama at 50\\%) to $|d|{=}7.44$ (Qwen at 25\\%), with Qwen at 5\\% ($|d|{=}4.15$) confirming detection at the lowest contamination level tested. Llama ($|d|{=}2.80$/$2.56$/$2.30$) and Mistral ($|d|{=}3.37$/$3.50$) show comparable magnitudes.\nOn ARC-C (295 items), Qwen yields $|d|{=}3.16$ at 15\\% and $|d|{=}5.30$ at 50\\%, demonstrating that detection remains feasible even on small benchmarks when contamination is sufficiently concentrated.\n\nClean-model $\\ell_z^*$ baselines diverge substantially across families: Qwen ${+}8.68$ ($\\text{SD}{=}0.74$), Mistral ${+}4.02$ ($\\text{SD}{=}2.05$), Llama ${+}0.26$ ($\\text{SD}{=}1.54$), and ARC-C Qwen ${-}5.42$ ($\\text{SD}{=}0.16$); this divergence reflects difficulty-ordering consistency rather than accuracy level (Appendix~\\ref{app:baseline_meaning}).\nAbsolute $\\ell_z^*$ thresholds would produce contradictory results: Qwen's contaminated $\\ell_z^*$ at 15\\% (${+}3.8$) is still positive.\nThe $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ design normalizes these divergent baselines: Cohen's $d$ yields comparable effect sizes ($|d|{=}2.30$--$7.44$) despite order-of-magnitude differences.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:heatmap} provides a visual summary.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cohen's $d$ for $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ across model families ($n{=}10$ clean seeds). Single-seed conditions: percentile bootstrap 95\\% CI (10,000 resamples). $^{\\dagger}$Multi-seed conditions: mean $\\pm$ SD ($n{=}3$ independent runs). All CIs exclude zero.}\n\\label{tab:cohens_d}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Bench. & $d$ (5\\%) [CI] & $d$ (15\\%) [CI] & $d$ (25\\%) [CI] & $d$ (50\\%) [CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^{\\dagger}$ & MMLU & $-4.15$ [$-9.9$, $-2.7$] & $-5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $-7.44$ ($\\pm$1.86) & $-5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\nLlama$^{\\dagger}$ & MMLU & --- & $-2.80$ ($\\pm$0.60) & $-2.56$ ($\\pm$0.52) & $-2.30$ ($\\pm$0.30) \\\\\nMistral & MMLU & --- & $-2.64$ [$-7.3$, $-1.5$] & --- & $-3.50$ [$-9.4$, $-2.1$] \\\\\nQwen$^{\\dagger}$ & ARC-C & --- & $+3.16$ [$2.0$, $9.5$] & --- & $+5.30$ [$3.6$, $15.2$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$Qwen and Llama MMLU 15\\%/25\\%/50\\%: multi-seed means ($n{=}3$ independent runs); Qwen 5\\%: single seed. Per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Cohen's $|d|$ heatmap (values from Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}; multi-seed means where available). Blue intensity reflects effect-size magnitude. Grey cells: conditions not tested.}\n  \\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Model-Specific $\\theta$-Shift Dynamics}\n\\label{sec:theta_dynamics}\n\nThe cross-family variation in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}---Qwen's $|d|$ declining from 25\\% to 50\\% while Llama's remains flat---suggests detection sensitivity depends on more than contamination dose.\nContamination raises overall accuracy, which inflates $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MLE}}$; a higher $\\hat{\\theta}$ ``explains away'' anomalous successes on hard items, absorbing the contamination signal into the ability estimate.\nWe term this \\emph{$\\theta$-shift absorption}.\nThe magnitude of $\\theta$-shift is comparable across families---Qwen ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ at 50\\%) and Llama ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$)---yet their fixed-$\\theta$ amplification diverges markedly (Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), indicating that $\\theta$-shift magnitude alone does not predict fixed-$\\theta$ benefit.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:theta_scatter} plots baseline accuracy against Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations.\n\n$\\theta$-shift absorption is the dominant source of detection variability for high-ability models at high contamination:\n(i) fixed-$\\theta$ estimation amplifies Qwen's multi-seed $|d|$ by 2.4$\\times$ at 50\\% ($5.08 \\to 12.32$; Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), while Llama shows no amplification ($0.4\\times$);\n(ii) $\\theta$-anchoring reduces Qwen's inter-seed CV from 60\\% to 19\\%, confirming that training-run variance is driven by $\\theta$-shift stochasticity rather than true signal variance;\n(iii) the cross-model ranking under free-$\\theta$ is seed-dependent at 50\\%, making $\\theta$-anchoring a necessary condition for reliable detection of high-ability models at high contamination.\n\nARC-C reverses this pattern.\nDespite Qwen's high baseline accuracy on ARC-C (89.7\\%), contamination produces larger, not smaller, effect sizes ($|d|{=}3.16$ at 15\\%, $|d|{=}5.30$ at 50\\%).\nThis reversal reflects a ceiling effect: with only 295 items and 89.7\\% baseline accuracy, the already-correct items offer minimal room for further $\\theta$ inflation, so contamination manifests primarily as anomalous successes on the few remaining hard items.\nThe small item count amplifies this concentration, producing a disproportionately large $\\ell_z^*$ shift per contaminated item.\n\nThe baseline $\\ell_z^*$ for ARC-C is negative ($-5.42$), reflecting the 4PL model's expectation of near-perfect performance; contamination pushes $\\ell_z^*$ toward zero, producing the positive Cohen's $d$ values in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}.\n\nThese findings indicate that detection sensitivity is not a fixed property of $\\ell_z^*$ but depends on the interaction between model ability and benchmark characteristics, a consideration absent from standard person-fit applications.\n\n\\paragraph{Fixed-$\\theta$ estimation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed} compares free-$\\theta$ and fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $d$ across all three families.\nThe benefit of fixed-$\\theta$ is modulated by baseline $\\ell_z^*$ level rather than $\\theta$-shift magnitude.\nQwen (67.8\\% accuracy, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}8.68$) shows 2.4$\\times$ amplification at 50\\%: its large baseline departure from the IRT null provides substantial signal that free-$\\theta$ absorption erodes, which $\\theta$-anchoring recovers (multi-seed mean ${\\ell_z^*}_{\\text{fixed}} {=} 14.5$ vs.\\ clean $7.35$).\nMistral (52.4\\%, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}4.02$) shows 1.7$\\times$ at 50\\%.\nLlama (59.4\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.39$ at 15\\%, $0.52$ at 50\\%) shows 0.4$\\times$ at 50\\% despite comparable $\\theta$-shift: its near-zero baseline $\\ell_z^*$ ($+0.26$) indicates that the clean response pattern conforms to IRT expectations, leaving no absorbed signal for $\\theta$-anchoring to recover.\nThe amplification ratio tracks baseline $|\\ell_z^*|$ monotonically ($8.68 {\\to} 2.4{\\times}$; $4.02 {\\to} 1.7{\\times}$; $0.26 {\\to} 0.4{\\times}$ at 50\\%), making fixed-$\\theta$ a targeted remedy for models with substantial baseline misfit, not a universal one.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Free-$\\theta$ vs.\\ fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $|d|$ on MMLU (bootstrap 95\\% CI). Fixed-$\\theta$ anchors $\\hat{\\theta}$ at the clean-model mean, eliminating $\\theta$-shift absorption.}\n\\label{tab:theta_fixed}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $|d_{\\text{free}}|$ & $|d_{\\text{fixed}}|$ & Ratio (fixed/free) & Fixed CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{5\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen & 4.15 & 3.52 & 0.8$\\times$ & [2.9, 5.7] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{15\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.36 & 6.85 & 1.3$\\times$ & ($\\pm$1.80) \\\\\nLlama & 2.11 & 0.58 & 0.3$\\times$ & [0, 2.37]$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 2.64 & 2.69 & 1.0$\\times$ & [1.53, 9.06] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{50\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.08 & 12.32 & 2.4$\\times$ & ($\\pm$2.38) \\\\\nLlama & 2.33 & 0.84 & 0.4$\\times$ & [0.17, 2.97] \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 3.50 & 5.80 & 1.7$\\times$ & [3.87, 18.45] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$CI includes zero.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\S}$Multi-seed mean ($n{=}3$); CI column shows $\\pm$SD of fixed-$\\theta$ $|d|$.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Fixed-$\\theta$: 6 seeds (4 response vectors from seeds 4--7 not retained in pipeline); free-$\\theta$ uses all 10.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_theta_scatter.pdf}\n  \\caption{Baseline accuracy vs.\\ Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations (single-seed values; multi-seed means in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}). Diamonds: 5\\%; triangles: 15\\%; squares: 25\\%; circles: 50\\%. ARC-C reversal reflects a ceiling effect.}\n  \\label{fig:theta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Detection-Diagnosis Tradeoff}\n\\label{sec:degradation}\n\nHaving established that $\\theta$-shift complicates detection, we ask whether the full 4PL framework is necessary, or whether simpler statistics suffice.\nTable~\\ref{tab:degradation} compares three statistics at decreasing levels of information access: $\\ell_z^*$ (requires 4PL parameters $a, b, c, d$), the HT statistic (requires only difficulty $b$), and an accuracy $z$-score (requires no item parameters).\n\nThe comparison reveals a detection-diagnosis tradeoff.\nFor pure detection, simpler statistics are more powerful: accuracy $z$-scores yield $|d|$ values several-fold to over an order of magnitude larger than person-fit statistics (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), because cross-seed accuracy variance is near-zero in controlled settings.\nHT generally exceeds $\\ell_z^*$ in detection strength, since HT is not attenuated by the Snijders correction for $\\theta$ estimation uncertainty.\nBoth person-fit statistics exhibit seed-dependent non-monotonicity under free-$\\theta$ estimation (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), confirming that $\\theta$-shift absorption affects all statistics conditioned on ability, not $\\ell_z^*$ specifically.\n\nYet the diagnostic power reverses.\nFull IRT parameterization is unnecessary for mere detection, but the value of $\\ell_z^*$ lies in three capabilities that HT and $z$-scores lack:\n(a) \\emph{seen/unseen decomposition}: $\\ell_z^*$ can be computed separately on seen and unseen item subsets, though item-level detection is near-random (AUROC $\\approx 0.53$; Appendix~\\ref{app:item_auroc}), confirming that the contamination signal is diffuse and requires model-level aggregation;\n(b) \\emph{$\\theta$-shift mechanism}: the ability parameter exposes why detection sensitivity depends on model ability (\\S\\ref{sec:theta_dynamics}), a mechanistic insight inaccessible to non-parametric statistics;\n(c) \\emph{structural sensitivity}: on real leaderboard data, $\\ell_z^*$ flags 57/60 model-benchmark cells as aberrant---evidence of systematic IRT assumption violations that motivates the $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ matched-difference design (\\S\\ref{sec:did}; Appendix~\\ref{app:leaderboard_aberrance}).\nAccuracy $z$-scores flag only 4/60 cells, making them insensitive to the response-pattern structure that $\\ell_z^*$ exploits for within-model diagnosis.\nWhile such patterns have theoretical antecedents in person-fit literature \\citep{stonge2011}, the specific magnitude and model-dependence we observe are new empirical findings in the LLM domain; nonparametric alternatives \\citep{tendeiro2014} may complement $\\ell_z^*$ where 4PL calibration is unavailable.\n\nIn practice, a two-stage workflow follows: screen using accuracy $z$-scores (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), then apply $\\ell_z^*$ to characterize whether the signal reflects $\\theta$-shift absorption (indicating fixed-$\\theta$ anchoring is needed) or uniform aberrance (suggesting direct memorization).\n\nWithin the IRT family, the 4PL specification matters: simulation ablations show a mean $+$27.3pp power gain over 3PL on MMLU, because 46\\% of MMLU items have upper asymptote $d < 0.95$, and the 3PL model (which assumes $d{=}1$) misattributes slip-related response patterns as aberrance (Appendix~\\ref{app:4pl_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection-diagnosis tradeoff on MMLU: Cohen's $|d|$ across three statistics. Simpler statistics detect more powerfully; $\\ell_z^*$ provides unique diagnostic capabilities (\\S\\ref{sec:degradation}).}\n\\label{tab:degradation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Statistic & $|d|$ (15\\%) & $|d|$ (50\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^\\dagger$ & $\\ell_z^*$ & $5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\n     & HT & $5.83$ ($\\pm$1.30) & $5.29$ ($\\pm$3.41) \\\\\n     & Acc.\\ $z$ & $45.0$ ($\\pm$2.5) & $70.0$ ($\\pm$1.1) \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLlama & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.11$ [$1.4$, $4.8$] & $2.33$ [$1.6$, $5.1$] \\\\\n      & HT & $2.45$ [$1.5$, $4.3$] & $3.07$ [$1.9$, $5.3$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $22.5$ [$13.6$, $47.0$] & $32.8$ [$19.8$, $68.6$] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.64$ [$1.5$, $7.3$] & $3.50$ [$2.1$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & HT & $4.36$ [$1.2$, $7.0$] & $5.89$ [$1.9$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $7.81$ [$4.4$, $14.4$] & $16.6$ [$9.7$, $34.6$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.9\\columnwidth}}{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$Qwen values are means across $n{=}3$ independent seeds (42, 123, 456); per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}. Llama and Mistral are single-seed with bootstrap CIs.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ability-Dependent Non-Monotonic Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:inverted_u}\n\nAs an exploratory observation, the free-$\\theta$ dose-response is non-monotonic for Qwen.\nThe multi-seed mean trajectory ($|d|$: $4.15 \\to 5.36 \\to 7.44 \\to 5.08$ at 5\\%/15\\%/25\\%/50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}) shows signal peaking at 25\\% before declining at 50\\%, but the decline is attenuated relative to single-seed estimates and the inter-seed variance at 50\\% is high (CV${=}$60\\%, range $[1.90, 7.91]$).\nThis non-monotonicity is a $\\theta$-estimation artifact: fixed-$\\theta$ analysis eliminates the inverted-U and reveals a strong positive dose-response (mean $|d|$: $5.89$ at 25\\% vs.\\ $12.32$ at 50\\%, no seed-level overlap), while reducing the 50\\% CV to 19\\%.\nLlama shows an approximately flat dose-response ($|d|{=}2.80 \\to 2.56 \\to 2.30$), consistent with its near-zero $\\ell_z^*$ baseline ($+0.26$ vs.\\ ${+}8.68$ for Qwen) despite comparable $\\theta$-shift ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$ vs.\\ $0.47$).\nThe mechanism is predictable from simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}): at moderate contamination, aberrant responses dominate; at high contamination, inflated $\\hat{\\theta}$ absorbs the anomaly---but the magnitude is highly seed-dependent.\nThree regimes are visible in Qwen's free-$\\theta$ trajectory: at 5\\%, $\\theta$-shift is negligible and $\\ell_z^*$ captures the full misfit; through 15--25\\%, pattern deviations outpace $\\theta$-shift absorption, yielding peak signal; at 50\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ absorbs enough aberrance that $|d|$ declines despite double the contamination fraction.\n\nA simulated power analysis corroborates these findings: two-sided testing (flagging both large negative and large positive $\\ell_z^*$) achieves ${>}$93\\% power on MMLU at all contamination levels, with the theoretical power function yielding $R^2 {=} 0.957$ against simulated values (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}).\n",

a higher $\\hat{\\theta}$ ``explains away'' anomalous successes on hard items, absorbing the contamination signal into the ability estimate.\nWe term this \\emph{$\\theta$-shift absorption}.\nThe magnitude of $\\theta$-shift is comparable across families---Qwen ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ at 50\\%) and Llama ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$)---yet their fixed-$\\theta$ amplification diverges markedly (Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), indicating that $\\theta$-shift magnitude alone does not predict fixed-$\\theta$ benefit.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:theta_scatter} plots baseline accuracy against Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations.\n\n$\\theta$-shift absorption is the dominant source of detection variability for high-ability models at high contamination:\n(i) fixed-$\\theta$ estimation amplifies Qwen's multi-seed $|d|$ by 2.4$\\times$ at 50\\% ($5.08 \\to 12.32$; Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), while Llama shows no amplification ($0.4\\times$);\n(ii) $\\theta$-anchoring reduces Qwen's inter-seed CV from 60\\% to 19\\%, confirming that training-run variance is driven by $\\theta$-shift stochasticity rather than true signal variance;\n(iii) the cross-model ranking under free-$\\theta$ is seed-dependent at 50\\%, making $\\theta$-anchoring a necessary condition for reliable detection of high-ability models at high contamination.\n\nARC-C reverses this pattern.\nDespite Qwen's high baseline accuracy on ARC-C (89.7\\%), contamination produces larger, not smaller, effect sizes ($|d|{=}3.16$ at 15\\%, $|d|{=}5.30$ at 50\\%).\nThis reversal reflects a ceiling effect: with only 295 items and 89.7\\% baseline accuracy, the already-correct items offer minimal room for further $\\theta$ inflation, so contamination manifests primarily as anomalous successes on the few remaining hard items.\nThe small item count amplifies this concentration, producing a disproportionately large $\\ell_z^*$ shift per contaminated item.\n\nThe baseline $\\ell_z^*$ for ARC-C is negative ($-5.42$), reflecting the 4PL model's expectation of near-perfect performance; contamination pushes $\\ell_z^*$ toward zero, producing the positive Cohen's $d$ values in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}.\n\nThese findings indicate that detection sensitivity is not a fixed property of $\\ell_z^*$ but depends on the interaction between model ability and benchmark characteristics, a consideration absent from standard person-fit applications.\n\n\\paragraph{Fixed-$\\theta$ estimation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed} compares free-$\\theta$ and fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $d$ across all three families.\nThe benefit of fixed-$\\theta$ is modulated by baseline $\\ell_z^*$ level rather than $\\theta$-shift magnitude.\nQwen (67.8\\% accuracy, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}8.68$) shows 2.4$\\times$ amplification at 50\\%: its large baseline departure from the IRT null provides substantial signal that free-$\\theta$ absorption erodes, which $\\theta$-anchoring recovers (multi-seed mean ${\\ell_z^*}_{\\text{fixed}} {=} 14.5$ vs.\\ clean $7.35$).\nMistral (52.4\\%, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}4.02$) shows 1.7$\\times$ at 50\\%.\nLlama (59.4\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.39$ at 15\\%, $0.52$ at 50\\%) shows 0.4$\\times$ at 50\\% despite comparable $\\theta$-shift: its near-zero baseline $\\ell_z^*$ ($+0.26$) indicates that the clean response pattern conforms to IRT expectations, leaving no absorbed signal for $\\theta$-anchoring to recover.\nThe amplification ratio tracks baseline $|\\ell_z^*|$ monotonically ($8.68 {\\to} 2.4{\\times}$; $4.02 {\\to} 1.7{\\times}$; $0.26 {\\to} 0.4{\\times}$ at 50\\%), making fixed-$\\theta$ a targeted remedy for models with substantial baseline misfit, not a universal one.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Free-$\\theta$ vs.\\ fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $|d|$ on MMLU (bootstrap 95\\% CI). Fixed-$\\theta$ anchors $\\hat{\\theta}$ at the clean-model mean, eliminating $\\theta$-shift absorption.}\n\\label{tab:theta_fixed}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $|d_{\\text{free}}|$ & $|d_{\\text{fixed}}|$ & Ratio (fixed/free) & Fixed CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{5\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen & 4.15 & 3.52 & 0.8$\\times$ & [2.9, 5.7] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{15\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.36 & 6.85 & 1.3$\\times$ & ($\\pm$1.80) \\\\\nLlama & 2.11 & 0.58 & 0.3$\\times$ & [0, 2.37]$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 2.64 & 2.69 & 1.0$\\times$ & [1.53, 9.06] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{50\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.08 & 12.32 & 2.4$\\times$ & ($\\pm$2.38) \\\\\nLlama & 2.33 & 0.84 & 0.4$\\times$ & [0.17, 2.97] \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 3.50 & 5.80 & 1.7$\\times$ & [3.87, 18.45] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$CI includes zero.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\S}$Multi-seed mean ($n{=}3$); CI column shows $\\pm$SD of fixed-$\\theta$ $|d|$.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Fixed-$\\theta$: 6 seeds (4 response vectors from seeds 4--7 not retained in pipeline); free-$\\theta$ uses all 10.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_theta_scatter.pdf}\n  \\caption{Baseline accuracy vs.\\ Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations (single-seed values; multi-seed means in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}). Diamonds: 5\\%; triangles: 15\\%; squares: 25\\%; circles: 50\\%. ARC-C reversal reflects a ceiling effect.}\n  \\label{fig:theta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Detection-Diagnosis Tradeoff}\n\\label{sec:degradation}\n\nHaving established that $\\theta$-shift complicates detection, we ask whether the full 4PL framework is necessary, or whether simpler statistics suffice.\nTable~\\ref{tab:degradation} compares three statistics at decreasing levels of information access: $\\ell_z^*$ (requires 4PL parameters $a, b, c, d$), the HT statistic (requires only difficulty $b$), and an accuracy $z$-score (requires no item parameters).\n\nThe comparison reveals a detection-diagnosis tradeoff.\nFor pure detection, simpler statistics are more powerful: accuracy $z$-scores yield $|d|$ values several-fold to over an order of magnitude larger than person-fit statistics (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), because cross-seed accuracy variance is near-zero in controlled settings.\nHT generally exceeds $\\ell_z^*$ in detection strength, since HT is not attenuated by the Snijders correction for $\\theta$ estimation uncertainty.\nBoth person-fit statistics exhibit seed-dependent non-monotonicity under free-$\\theta$ estimation (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), confirming that $\\theta$-shift absorption affects all statistics conditioned on ability, not $\\ell_z^*$ specifically.\n\nYet the diagnostic power reverses.\nFull IRT parameterization is unnecessary for mere detection, but the value of $\\ell_z^*$ lies in three capabilities that HT and $z$-scores lack:\n(a) \\emph{seen/unseen decomposition}: $\\ell_z^*$ can be computed separately on seen and unseen item subsets, though item-level detection is near-random (AUROC $\\approx 0.53$; Appendix~\\ref{app:item_auroc}), confirming that the contamination signal is diffuse and requires model-level aggregation;\n(b) \\emph{$\\theta$-shift mechanism}: the ability parameter exposes why detection sensitivity depends on model ability (\\S\\ref{sec:theta_dynamics}), a mechanistic insight inaccessible to non-parametric statistics;\n(c) \\emph{structural sensitivity}: on real leaderboard data, $\\ell_z^*$ flags 57/60 model-benchmark cells as aberrant---evidence of systematic IRT assumption violations that motivates the $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ matched-difference design (\\S\\ref{sec:did}; Appendix~\\ref{app:leaderboard_aberrance}).\nAccuracy $z$-scores flag only 4/60 cells, making them insensitive to the response-pattern structure that $\\ell_z^*$ exploits for within-model diagnosis.\nWhile such patterns have theoretical antecedents in person-fit literature \\citep{stonge2011}, the specific magnitude and model-dependence we observe are new empirical findings in the LLM domain; nonparametric alternatives \\citep{tendeiro2014} may complement $\\ell_z^*$ where 4PL calibration is unavailable.\n\nIn practice, a two-stage workflow follows: screen using accuracy $z$-scores (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), then apply $\\ell_z^*$ to characterize whether the signal reflects $\\theta$-shift absorption (indicating fixed-$\\theta$ anchoring is needed) or uniform aberrance (suggesting direct memorization).\n\nWithin the IRT family, the 4PL specification matters: simulation ablations show a mean $+$27.3pp power gain over 3PL on MMLU, because 46\\% of MMLU items have upper asymptote $d < 0.95$, and the 3PL model (which assumes $d{=}1$) misattributes slip-related response patterns as aberrance (Appendix~\\ref{app:4pl_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection-diagnosis tradeoff on MMLU: Cohen's $|d|$ across three statistics. Simpler statistics detect more powerfully; $\\ell_z^*$ provides unique diagnostic capabilities (\\S\\ref{sec:degradation}).}\n\\label{tab:degradation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Statistic & $|d|$ (15\\%) & $|d|$ (50\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^\\dagger$ & $\\ell_z^*$ & $5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\n     & HT & $5.83$ ($\\pm$1.30) & $5.29$ ($\\pm$3.41) \\\\\n     & Acc.\\ $z$ & $45.0$ ($\\pm$2.5) & $70.0$ ($\\pm$1.1) \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLlama & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.11$ [$1.4$, $4.8$] & $2.33$ [$1.6$, $5.1$] \\\\\n      & HT & $2.45$ [$1.5$, $4.3$] & $3.07$ [$1.9$, $5.3$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $22.5$ [$13.6$, $47.0$] & $32.8$ [$19.8$, $68.6$] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.64$ [$1.5$, $7.3$] & $3.50$ [$2.1$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & HT & $4.36$ [$1.2$, $7.0$] & $5.89$ [$1.9$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $7.81$ [$4.4$, $14.4$] & $16.6$ [$9.7$, $34.6$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.9\\columnwidth}}{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$Qwen values are means across $n{=}3$ independent seeds (42, 123, 456); per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}. Llama and Mistral are single-seed with bootstrap CIs.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ability-Dependent Non-Monotonic Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:inverted_u}\n\nAs an exploratory observation, the free-$\\theta$ dose-response is non-monotonic for Qwen.\nThe multi-seed mean trajectory ($|d|$: $4.15 \\to 5.36 \\to 7.44 \\to 5.08$ at 5\\%/15\\%/25\\%/50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}) shows signal peaking at 25\\% before declining at 50\\%, but the decline is attenuated relative to single-seed estimates and the inter-seed variance at 50\\% is high (CV${=}$60\\%, range $[1.90, 7.91]$).\nThis non-monotonicity is a $\\theta$-estimation artifact: fixed-$\\theta$ analysis eliminates the inverted-U and reveals a strong positive dose-response (mean $|d|$: $5.89$ at 25\\% vs.\\ $12.32$ at 50\\%, no seed-level overlap), while reducing the 50\\% CV to 19\\%.\nLlama shows an approximately flat dose-response ($|d|{=}2.80 \\to 2.56 \\to 2.30$), consistent with its near-zero $\\ell_z^*$ baseline ($+0.26$ vs.\\ ${+}8.68$ for Qwen) despite comparable $\\theta$-shift ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$ vs.\\ $0.47$).\nThe mechanism is predictable from simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}): at moderate contamination, aberrant responses dominate; at high contamination, inflated $\\hat{\\theta}$ absorbs the anomaly---but the magnitude is highly seed-dependent.\nThree regimes are visible in Qwen's free-$\\theta$ trajectory: at 5\\%, $\\theta$-shift is negligible and $\\ell_z^*$ captures the full misfit; through 15--25\\%, pattern deviations outpace $\\theta$-shift absorption, yielding peak signal; at 50\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ absorbs enough aberrance that $|d|$ declines despite double the contamination fraction.\n\nA simulated power analysis corroborates these findings: two-sided testing (flagging both large negative and large positive $\\ell_z^*$) achieves ${>}$93\\% power on MMLU at all contamination levels, with the theoretical power function yielding $R^2 {=} 0.957$ against simulated values (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}).\n",

Fixed-$\\theta$ anchors $\\hat{\\theta}$ at the clean-model mean, eliminating $\\theta$-shift absorption.}\n\\label{tab:theta_fixed}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & $|d_{\\text{free}}|$ & $|d_{\\text{fixed}}|$ & Ratio (fixed/free) & Fixed CI \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{5\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen & 4.15 & 3.52 & 0.8$\\times$ & [2.9, 5.7] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{15\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.36 & 6.85 & 1.3$\\times$ & ($\\pm$1.80) \\\\\nLlama & 2.11 & 0.58 & 0.3$\\times$ & [0, 2.37]$^{\\dagger}$ \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 2.64 & 2.69 & 1.0$\\times$ & [1.53, 9.06] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\textit{50\\% contamination}} \\\\\nQwen$^{\\S}$ & 5.08 & 12.32 & 2.4$\\times$ & ($\\pm$2.38) \\\\\nLlama & 2.33 & 0.84 & 0.4$\\times$ & [0.17, 2.97] \\\\\nMistral$^{\\ddagger}$ & 3.50 & 5.80 & 1.7$\\times$ & [3.87, 18.45] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\dagger}$CI includes zero.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\S}$Multi-seed mean ($n{=}3$); CI column shows $\\pm$SD of fixed-$\\theta$ $|d|$.} \\\\\n\\multicolumn{5}{@{}l}{\\scriptsize $^{\\ddagger}$Fixed-$\\theta$: 6 seeds (4 response vectors from seeds 4--7 not retained in pipeline); free-$\\theta$ uses all 10.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig_theta_scatter.pdf}\n  \\caption{Baseline accuracy vs.\\ Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations (single-seed values; multi-seed means in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}). Diamonds: 5\\%; triangles: 15\\%; squares: 25\\%; circles: 50\\%. ARC-C reversal reflects a ceiling effect.}\n  \\label{fig:theta_scatter}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Detection-Diagnosis Tradeoff}\n\\label{sec:degradation}\n\nHaving established that $\\theta$-shift complicates detection, we ask whether the full 4PL framework is necessary, or whether simpler statistics suffice.\nTable~\\ref{tab:degradation} compares three statistics at decreasing levels of information access: $\\ell_z^*$ (requires 4PL parameters $a, b, c, d$), the HT statistic (requires only difficulty $b$), and an accuracy $z$-score (requires no item parameters).\n\nThe comparison reveals a detection-diagnosis tradeoff.\nFor pure detection, simpler statistics are more powerful: accuracy $z$-scores yield $|d|$ values several-fold to over an order of magnitude larger than person-fit statistics (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), because cross-seed accuracy variance is near-zero in controlled settings.\nHT generally exceeds $\\ell_z^*$ in detection strength, since HT is not attenuated by the Snijders correction for $\\theta$ estimation uncertainty.\nBoth person-fit statistics exhibit seed-dependent non-monotonicity under free-$\\theta$ estimation (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), confirming that $\\theta$-shift absorption affects all statistics conditioned on ability, not $\\ell_z^*$ specifically.\n\nYet the diagnostic power reverses.\nFull IRT parameterization is unnecessary for mere detection, but the value of $\\ell_z^*$ lies in three capabilities that HT and $z$-scores lack:\n(a) \\emph{seen/unseen decomposition}: $\\ell_z^*$ can be computed separately on seen and unseen item subsets, though item-level detection is near-random (AUROC $\\approx 0.53$; Appendix~\\ref{app:item_auroc}), confirming that the contamination signal is diffuse and requires model-level aggregation;\n(b) \\emph{$\\theta$-shift mechanism}: the ability parameter exposes why detection sensitivity depends on model ability (\\S\\ref{sec:theta_dynamics}), a mechanistic insight inaccessible to non-parametric statistics;\n(c) \\emph{structural sensitivity}: on real leaderboard data, $\\ell_z^*$ flags 57/60 model-benchmark cells as aberrant---evidence of systematic IRT assumption violations that motivates the $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ matched-difference design (\\S\\ref{sec:did}; Appendix~\\ref{app:leaderboard_aberrance}).\nAccuracy $z$-scores flag only 4/60 cells, making them insensitive to the response-pattern structure that $\\ell_z^*$ exploits for within-model diagnosis.\nWhile such patterns have theoretical antecedents in person-fit literature \\citep{stonge2011}, the specific magnitude and model-dependence we observe are new empirical findings in the LLM domain; nonparametric alternatives \\citep{tendeiro2014} may complement $\\ell_z^*$ where 4PL calibration is unavailable.\n\nIn practice, a two-stage workflow follows: screen using accuracy $z$-scores (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), then apply $\\ell_z^*$ to characterize whether the signal reflects $\\theta$-shift absorption (indicating fixed-$\\theta$ anchoring is needed) or uniform aberrance (suggesting direct memorization).\n\nWithin the IRT family, the 4PL specification matters: simulation ablations show a mean $+$27.3pp power gain over 3PL on MMLU, because 46\\% of MMLU items have upper asymptote $d < 0.95$, and the 3PL model (which assumes $d{=}1$) misattributes slip-related response patterns as aberrance (Appendix~\\ref{app:4pl_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection-diagnosis tradeoff on MMLU: Cohen's $|d|$ across three statistics. Simpler statistics detect more powerfully; $\\ell_z^*$ provides unique diagnostic capabilities (\\S\\ref{sec:degradation}).}\n\\label{tab:degradation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Statistic & $|d|$ (15\\%) & $|d|$ (50\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^\\dagger$ & $\\ell_z^*$ & $5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\n     & HT & $5.83$ ($\\pm$1.30) & $5.29$ ($\\pm$3.41) \\\\\n     & Acc.\\ $z$ & $45.0$ ($\\pm$2.5) & $70.0$ ($\\pm$1.1) \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLlama & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.11$ [$1.4$, $4.8$] & $2.33$ [$1.6$, $5.1$] \\\\\n      & HT & $2.45$ [$1.5$, $4.3$] & $3.07$ [$1.9$, $5.3$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $22.5$ [$13.6$, $47.0$] & $32.8$ [$19.8$, $68.6$] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.64$ [$1.5$, $7.3$] & $3.50$ [$2.1$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & HT & $4.36$ [$1.2$, $7.0$] & $5.89$ [$1.9$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $7.81$ [$4.4$, $14.4$] & $16.6$ [$9.7$, $34.6$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.9\\columnwidth}}{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$Qwen values are means across $n{=}3$ independent seeds (42, 123, 456); per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}. Llama and Mistral are single-seed with bootstrap CIs.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ability-Dependent Non-Monotonic Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:inverted_u}\n\nAs an exploratory observation, the free-$\\theta$ dose-response is non-monotonic for Qwen.\nThe multi-seed mean trajectory ($|d|$: $4.15 \\to 5.36 \\to 7.44 \\to 5.08$ at 5\\%/15\\%/25\\%/50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}) shows signal peaking at 25\\% before declining at 50\\%, but the decline is attenuated relative to single-seed estimates and the inter-seed variance at 50\\% is high (CV${=}$60\\%, range $[1.90, 7.91]$).\nThis non-monotonicity is a $\\theta$-estimation artifact: fixed-$\\theta$ analysis eliminates the inverted-U and reveals a strong positive dose-response (mean $|d|$: $5.89$ at 25\\% vs.\\ $12.32$ at 50\\%, no seed-level overlap), while reducing the 50\\% CV to 19\\%.\nLlama shows an approximately flat dose-response ($|d|{=}2.80 \\to 2.56 \\to 2.30$), consistent with its near-zero $\\ell_z^*$ baseline ($+0.26$ vs.\\ ${+}8.68$ for Qwen) despite comparable $\\theta$-shift ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$ vs.\\ $0.47$).\nThe mechanism is predictable from simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}): at moderate contamination, aberrant responses dominate; at high contamination, inflated $\\hat{\\theta}$ absorbs the anomaly---but the magnitude is highly seed-dependent.\nThree regimes are visible in Qwen's free-$\\theta$ trajectory: at 5\\%, $\\theta$-shift is negligible and $\\ell_z^*$ captures the full misfit; through 15--25\\%, pattern deviations outpace $\\theta$-shift absorption, yielding peak signal; at 50\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ absorbs enough aberrance that $|d|$ declines despite double the contamination fraction.\n\nA simulated power analysis corroborates these findings: two-sided testing (flagging both large negative and large positive $\\ell_z^*$) achieves ${>}$93\\% power on MMLU at all contamination levels, with the theoretical power function yielding $R^2 {=} 0.957$ against simulated values (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}).\n",

Appendix~\\ref{app:item_auroc}), confirming that the contamination signal is diffuse and requires model-level aggregation;\n(b) \\emph{$\\theta$-shift mechanism}: the ability parameter exposes why detection sensitivity depends on model ability (\\S\\ref{sec:theta_dynamics}), a mechanistic insight inaccessible to non-parametric statistics;\n(c) \\emph{structural sensitivity}: on real leaderboard data, $\\ell_z^*$ flags 57/60 model-benchmark cells as aberrant---evidence of systematic IRT assumption violations that motivates the $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ matched-difference design (\\S\\ref{sec:did}; Appendix~\\ref{app:leaderboard_aberrance}).\nAccuracy $z$-scores flag only 4/60 cells, making them insensitive to the response-pattern structure that $\\ell_z^*$ exploits for within-model diagnosis.\nWhile such patterns have theoretical antecedents in person-fit literature \\citep{stonge2011}, the specific magnitude and model-dependence we observe are new empirical findings in the LLM domain; nonparametric alternatives \\citep{tendeiro2014} may complement $\\ell_z^*$ where 4PL calibration is unavailable.\n\nIn practice, a two-stage workflow follows: screen using accuracy $z$-scores (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), then apply $\\ell_z^*$ to characterize whether the signal reflects $\\theta$-shift absorption (indicating fixed-$\\theta$ anchoring is needed) or uniform aberrance (suggesting direct memorization).\n\nWithin the IRT family, the 4PL specification matters: simulation ablations show a mean $+$27.3pp power gain over 3PL on MMLU, because 46\\% of MMLU items have upper asymptote $d < 0.95$, and the 3PL model (which assumes $d{=}1$) misattributes slip-related response patterns as aberrance (Appendix~\\ref{app:4pl_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection-diagnosis tradeoff on MMLU: Cohen's $|d|$ across three statistics. Simpler statistics detect more powerfully; $\\ell_z^*$ provides unique diagnostic capabilities (\\S\\ref{sec:degradation}).}\n\\label{tab:degradation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Statistic & $|d|$ (15\\%) & $|d|$ (50\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^\\dagger$ & $\\ell_z^*$ & $5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\n     & HT & $5.83$ ($\\pm$1.30) & $5.29$ ($\\pm$3.41) \\\\\n     & Acc.\\ $z$ & $45.0$ ($\\pm$2.5) & $70.0$ ($\\pm$1.1) \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLlama & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.11$ [$1.4$, $4.8$] & $2.33$ [$1.6$, $5.1$] \\\\\n      & HT & $2.45$ [$1.5$, $4.3$] & $3.07$ [$1.9$, $5.3$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $22.5$ [$13.6$, $47.0$] & $32.8$ [$19.8$, $68.6$] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.64$ [$1.5$, $7.3$] & $3.50$ [$2.1$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & HT & $4.36$ [$1.2$, $7.0$] & $5.89$ [$1.9$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $7.81$ [$4.4$, $14.4$] & $16.6$ [$9.7$, $34.6$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.9\\columnwidth}}{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$Qwen values are means across $n{=}3$ independent seeds (42, 123, 456); per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}. Llama and Mistral are single-seed with bootstrap CIs.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ability-Dependent Non-Monotonic Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:inverted_u}\n\nAs an exploratory observation, the free-$\\theta$ dose-response is non-monotonic for Qwen.\nThe multi-seed mean trajectory ($|d|$: $4.15 \\to 5.36 \\to 7.44 \\to 5.08$ at 5\\%/15\\%/25\\%/50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}) shows signal peaking at 25\\% before declining at 50\\%, but the decline is attenuated relative to single-seed estimates and the inter-seed variance at 50\\% is high (CV${=}$60\\%, range $[1.90, 7.91]$).\nThis non-monotonicity is a $\\theta$-estimation artifact: fixed-$\\theta$ analysis eliminates the inverted-U and reveals a strong positive dose-response (mean $|d|$: $5.89$ at 25\\% vs.\\ $12.32$ at 50\\%, no seed-level overlap), while reducing the 50\\% CV to 19\\%.\nLlama shows an approximately flat dose-response ($|d|{=}2.80 \\to 2.56 \\to 2.30$), consistent with its near-zero $\\ell_z^*$ baseline ($+0.26$ vs.\\ ${+}8.68$ for Qwen) despite comparable $\\theta$-shift ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$ vs.\\ $0.47$).\nThe mechanism is predictable from simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}): at moderate contamination, aberrant responses dominate; at high contamination, inflated $\\hat{\\theta}$ absorbs the anomaly---but the magnitude is highly seed-dependent.\nThree regimes are visible in Qwen's free-$\\theta$ trajectory: at 5\\%, $\\theta$-shift is negligible and $\\ell_z^*$ captures the full misfit; through 15--25\\%, pattern deviations outpace $\\theta$-shift absorption, yielding peak signal; at 50\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ absorbs enough aberrance that $|d|$ declines despite double the contamination fraction.\n\nA simulated power analysis corroborates these findings: two-sided testing (flagging both large negative and large positive $\\ell_z^*$) achieves ${>}$93\\% power on MMLU at all contamination levels, with the theoretical power function yielding $R^2 {=} 0.957$ against simulated values (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Across Model Families}\n\\label{sec:detection}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:cohens_d} presents Cohen's $d$ effect sizes for $\\ell_z^*$ across three model families and two benchmarks.\nAll confidence intervals exclude zero, confirming that $\\Delta\\ell_z^*$ reliably separates contaminated from clean models at both contamination levels.\n\nOn MMLU, effect sizes range from $|d|{=}2.30$ (Llama at 50\\%) to $|d|{=}7.44$ (Qwen at 25\\%), with Qwen at 5\\% ($|d|{=}4.15$) confirming detection at the lowest contamination level tested. Single-seed conditions: percentile bootstrap 95\\% CI (10,000 resamples). $^{\\dagger}$Multi-seed conditions: mean $\\pm$ SD ($n{=}3$ independent runs). Per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}.}\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Cohen's $|d|$ heatmap (values from Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}; multi-seed means where available). Blue intensity reflects effect-size magnitude. Grey cells: conditions not tested.}\n  \\label{fig:heatmap}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\subsection{Model-Specific $\\theta$-Shift Dynamics}\n\\label{sec:theta_dynamics}\n\nThe cross-family variation in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}---Qwen's $|d|$ declining from 25\\% to 50\\% while Llama's remains flat---suggests detection sensitivity depends on more than contamination dose.\nContamination raises overall accuracy, which inflates $\\hat{\\theta}_{\\text{MLE}}$; a higher $\\hat{\\theta}$ ``explains away'' anomalous successes on hard items, absorbing the contamination signal into the ability estimate.\nWe term this \\emph{$\\theta$-shift absorption}.\nThe magnitude of $\\theta$-shift is comparable across families---Qwen ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ at 50\\%) and Llama ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$)---yet their fixed-$\\theta$ amplification diverges markedly (Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), indicating that $\\theta$-shift magnitude alone does not predict fixed-$\\theta$ benefit.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:theta_scatter} plots baseline accuracy against Cohen's $|d|$ for all model-benchmark combinations.\n\n$\\theta$-shift absorption is the dominant source of detection variability for high-ability models at high contamination:\n(i) fixed-$\\theta$ estimation amplifies Qwen's multi-seed $|d|$ by 2.4$\\times$ at 50\\% ($5.08 \\to 12.32$; Table~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed}), while Llama shows no amplification ($0.4\\times$);\n(ii) $\\theta$-anchoring reduces Qwen's inter-seed CV from 60\\% to 19\\%, confirming that training-run variance is driven by $\\theta$-shift stochasticity rather than true signal variance;\n(iii) the cross-model ranking under free-$\\theta$ is seed-dependent at 50\\%, making $\\theta$-anchoring a necessary condition for reliable detection of high-ability models at high contamination.\n\nARC-C reverses this pattern.\nDespite Qwen's high baseline accuracy on ARC-C (89.7\\%), contamination produces larger, not smaller, effect sizes ($|d|{=}3.16$ at 15\\%, $|d|{=}5.30$ at 50\\%).\nThis reversal reflects a ceiling effect: with only 295 items and 89.7\\% baseline accuracy, the already-correct items offer minimal room for further $\\theta$ inflation, so contamination manifests primarily as anomalous successes on the few remaining hard items.\nThe small item count amplifies this concentration, producing a disproportionately large $\\ell_z^*$ shift per contaminated item.\n\nThe baseline $\\ell_z^*$ for ARC-C is negative ($-5.42$), reflecting the 4PL model's expectation of near-perfect performance; contamination pushes $\\ell_z^*$ toward zero, producing the positive Cohen's $d$ values in Table~\\ref{tab:cohens_d}.\n\nThese findings indicate that detection sensitivity is not a fixed property of $\\ell_z^*$ but depends on the interaction between model ability and benchmark characteristics, a consideration absent from standard person-fit applications.\n\n\\paragraph{Fixed-$\\theta$ estimation.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_fixed} compares free-$\\theta$ and fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $d$ across all three families.\nThe benefit of fixed-$\\theta$ is modulated by baseline $\\ell_z^*$ level rather than $\\theta$-shift magnitude.\nQwen (67.8\\% accuracy, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}8.68$) shows 2.4$\\times$ amplification at 50\\%: its large baseline departure from the IRT null provides substantial signal that free-$\\theta$ absorption erodes, which $\\theta$-anchoring recovers (multi-seed mean ${\\ell_z^*}_{\\text{fixed}} {=} 14.5$ vs.\\ clean $7.35$).\nMistral (52.4\\%, baseline $\\ell_z^*{=}{+}4.02$) shows 1.7$\\times$ at 50\\%.\nLlama (59.4\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.39$ at 15\\%, $0.52$ at 50\\%) shows 0.4$\\times$ at 50\\% despite comparable $\\theta$-shift: its near-zero baseline $\\ell_z^*$ ($+0.26$) indicates that the clean response pattern conforms to IRT expectations, leaving no absorbed signal for $\\theta$-anchoring to recover.\nThe amplification ratio tracks baseline $|\\ell_z^*|$ monotonically ($8.68 {\\to} 2.4{\\times}$; $4.02 {\\to} 1.7{\\times}$; $0.26 {\\to} 0.4{\\times}$ at 50\\%), making fixed-$\\theta$ a targeted remedy for models with substantial baseline misfit, not a universal one.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Free-$\\theta$ vs.\\ fixed-$\\theta$ Cohen's $|d|$ on MMLU (bootstrap 95\\% CI). nonparametric alternatives \\citep{tendeiro2014} may complement $\\ell_z^*$ where 4PL calibration is unavailable.\n\nIn practice, a two-stage workflow follows: screen using accuracy $z$-scores (Table~\\ref{tab:degradation}), then apply $\\ell_z^*$ to characterize whether the signal reflects $\\theta$-shift absorption (indicating fixed-$\\theta$ anchoring is needed) or uniform aberrance (suggesting direct memorization).\n\nWithin the IRT family, the 4PL specification matters: simulation ablations show a mean $+$27.3pp power gain over 3PL on MMLU, because 46\\% of MMLU items have upper asymptote $d < 0.95$, and the 3PL model (which assumes $d{=}1$) misattributes slip-related response patterns as aberrance (Appendix~\\ref{app:4pl_ablation}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Detection-diagnosis tradeoff on MMLU: Cohen's $|d|$ across three statistics. Simpler statistics detect more powerfully; $\\ell_z^*$ provides unique diagnostic capabilities (\\S\\ref{sec:degradation}).}\n\\label{tab:degradation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Statistic & $|d|$ (15\\%) & $|d|$ (50\\%) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen$^\\dagger$ & $\\ell_z^*$ & $5.36$ ($\\pm$1.10) & $5.08$ ($\\pm$3.02) \\\\\n     & HT & $5.83$ ($\\pm$1.30) & $5.29$ ($\\pm$3.41) \\\\\n     & Acc.\\ $z$ & $45.0$ ($\\pm$2.5) & $70.0$ ($\\pm$1.1) \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nLlama & $\\ell_z^*$ & $2.11$ [$1.4$, $4.8$] & $2.33$ [$1.6$, $5.1$] \\\\\n      & HT & $2.45$ [$1.5$, $4.3$] & $3.07$ [$1.9$, $5.3$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $22.5$ [$13.6$, $47.0$] & $32.8$ [$19.8$, $68.6$] \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral$^\\dagger$ & $\\ell_z^*$ & $3.37$ ($\\pm$0.68) & $3.50$ [$2.1$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & HT & $4.36$ [$1.2$, $7.0$] & $5.89$ [$1.9$, $9.4$] \\\\\n      & Acc.\\ $z$ & $7.81$ [$4.4$, $14.4$] & $16.6$ [$9.7$, $34.6$] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{@{}p{0.9\\columnwidth}}{\\scriptsize $^\\dagger$Qwen values are means across $n{=}3$ independent seeds (42, 123, 456); per-seed values in Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}. Llama and Mistral are single-seed with bootstrap CIs.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Ability-Dependent Non-Monotonic Dose-Response}\n\\label{sec:inverted_u}\n\nAs an exploratory observation, the free-$\\theta$ dose-response is non-monotonic for Qwen.\nThe multi-seed mean trajectory ($|d|$: $4.15 \\to 5.36 \\to 7.44 \\to 5.08$ at 5\\%/15\\%/25\\%/50\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:reproducibility}) shows signal peaking at 25\\% before declining at 50\\%, but the decline is attenuated relative to single-seed estimates and the inter-seed variance at 50\\% is high (CV${=}$60\\%, range $[1.90, 7.91]$).\nThis non-monotonicity is a $\\theta$-estimation artifact: fixed-$\\theta$ analysis eliminates the inverted-U and reveals a strong positive dose-response (mean $|d|$: $5.89$ at 25\\% vs.\\ $12.32$ at 50\\%, no seed-level overlap), while reducing the 50\\% CV to 19\\%.\nLlama shows an approximately flat dose-response ($|d|{=}2.80 \\to 2.56 \\to 2.30$), consistent with its near-zero $\\ell_z^*$ baseline ($+0.26$ vs.\\ ${+}8.68$ for Qwen) despite comparable $\\theta$-shift ($\\Delta\\theta{=}0.52$ vs.\\ $0.47$).\nThe mechanism is predictable from simulation (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}): at moderate contamination, aberrant responses dominate; at high contamination, inflated $\\hat{\\theta}$ absorbs the anomaly---but the magnitude is highly seed-dependent.\nThree regimes are visible in Qwen's free-$\\theta$ trajectory: at 5\\%, $\\theta$-shift is negligible and $\\ell_z^*$ captures the full misfit; through 15--25\\%, pattern deviations outpace $\\theta$-shift absorption, yielding peak signal; at 50\\%, $\\Delta\\theta{=}0.47$ absorbs enough aberrance that $|d|$ declines despite double the contamination fraction.\n\nA simulated power analysis corroborates these findings: two-sided testing (flagging both large negative and large positive $\\ell_z^*$) achieves ${>}$93\\% power on MMLU at all contamination levels, with the theoretical power function yielding $R^2 {=} 0.957$ against simulated values (Appendix~\\ref{app:simulation}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur controlled experiments cover three 7--8B model families with QLoRA fine-tuning; generalization to larger scales, mixture-of-experts architectures, full-parameter fine-tuning, and pretraining-stage contamination remains untested.\nMost contamination conditions use a single training run, and multi-seed replication (available for Qwen and Llama) reveals high seed dependence at 50\\% contamination (CV${=}$60\\%), partially resolved by $\\theta$-anchoring; the post-RL characterization is $N{=}1$.\nThe framework depends on pre-calibrated 4PL item parameters from PSN-IRT, whose calibration sample may include contaminated models, and we do not provide head-to-head comparison with logit-based methods (MIA, ConStat) that operate at a different information level.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HYCSbzfEwaTbCp6M8ppU2h`</summary>

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	
3	Our controlled experiments cover three 7--8B model families with QLoRA fine-tuning; generalization to larger scales, mixture-of-experts architectures, full-parameter fine-tuning, and pretraining-stage contamination remains untested.
4	Most contamination conditions use a single training run, and multi-seed replication (available for Qwen and Llama) reveals high seed dependence at 50\% contamination (CV${=}$60\%), partially resolved by $\theta$-anchoring; the post-RL characterization is $N{=}1$.
5	The framework depends on pre-calibrated 4PL item parameters from PSN-IRT, whose calibration sample may include contaminated models, and we do not provide head-to-head comparison with logit-based methods (MIA, ConStat) that operate at a different information level.
6	
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur controlled experiments cover three 7--8B model families with QLoRA fine-tuning; generalization to larger scales, mixture-of-experts architectures, full-parameter fine-tuning, and pretraining-stage contamination remains untested.\nMost contamination conditions use a single training run, and multi-seed replication (available for Qwen and Llama) reveals high seed dependence at 50\\% contamination (CV${=}$60\\%), partially resolved by $\\theta$-anchoring; the post-RL characterization is $N{=}1$.\nThe framework depends on pre-calibrated 4PL item parameters from PSN-IRT, whose calibration sample may include contaminated models, and we do not provide head-to-head comparison with logit-based methods (MIA, ConStat) that operate at a different information level.\n",
